--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The mission is clear: **before the switch, robots should be STATIONARY (Stop); after the switch, robots should be MOVING**. The switch is signaled by a GREEN wall lighting up.

The champion is simply `--s0 1` (Stop always) scoring 10032.20. This works because:
- Before switch (~500 steps avg): robots are stopped → no penalty
- After switch (~700 steps avg): robots are stopped → penalty accumulates

The ideal strategy:
1. Start in **Stop** state
2. When the GREEN wall appears (detected via C=7 with color 2=green), switch to **Exploration/GoToColor**

The floor is uniformly GRAY, so floor color conditions won't help distinguish phases. The key perception is **seeing green** (color 2) via condition C=7.

**Design:**
- State 0: Stop (B=1) → transition to moving state when green is perceived (C=7, l=2)
- State 1: Exploration (B=0) or GoToColor green (B=8) → keep moving

Let me design 14 machines:

1. **Champion (exact copy)**: 1-state Stop
2-5. **2-state: Stop → Explore on green**: variations with different probabilities and movement behaviors
6-8. **2-state: Stop → GoToColor(green)**: move toward the green wall
9-11. **3-state machines**: Stop → Explore, with fallback
12-14. **Exploration variants**: different topologies

Key insight: After seeing green, robots should MOVE. GoToColor green (B=8, clr=2) is ideal as it moves toward the green wall signal.

FSM: --nstates 1 --s0 1 --cle0 0

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Key observations from rounds 1-2:

1. **Champion**: Simple 1-state Stop (B=1) scores ~9929. This is hard to beat because stopping before the switch is optimal, and the cost of moving after the switch must be weighed against the benefit of stopping before.

2. **2nd place**: Stop → Explore on green (C=7, l=2, p=0.95), then Explore (rwm=70) with very low prob of returning (p=0.002) and also transition on "neighbors below 1" (C=4, p=1, w=5). Score 10428 - worse than champion.

3. The pattern is clear: **the simple Stop machine is near-optimal**. The 2-state machines that transition to movement on seeing green are WORSE than just staying stopped, because:
   - Robots can't always see the green wall (range-and-bearing limited, camera may not see walls at all angles)
   - The probability of triggering on green when it's NOT there (false positives) costs score before the switch
   - The score after switch with moving ~700 steps × 20 robots = 14000 potential, vs staying stopped = same

4. The problem is: after the switch, stopped robots accumulate score. With 700 steps after switch and 20 robots all stopped = 14000. With 1-state Stop, the actual score ~9929 suggests the switch happens around step 496 on average, giving ~496×0 (stopped before) + 704×20 = 14080... but the score is 9929, which means not all steps after switch are counted fully? Or the switch timing variance helps.

Wait - let me reconsider. Score 9929 means ~9929 robot-steps of penalty. With all robots stopped always: penalty = steps_after_switch × 20. If switch is uniform [400,600], expected = 500 steps after switch... no, 1200-500 = 700 steps × 20 = 14000. But score is 9929, suggesting something else is happening.

Actually re-reading: robots start scattered and may move slightly? Or maybe the Stop behavior has some residual movement? The score 9929 < 14000 is puzzling. Maybe some robots stop before the switch and some don't register as moving.

The 2-state machines with green detection score ~10428-10749, which are WORSE. This means detecting green and moving after is not better than staying stopped the whole time.

**New insight**: The optimal strategy might be to MOVE before the switch (to avoid penalty for being stationary... wait no - before switch, penalty is for STATIONARY robots). So:
- Before switch: penalty for STATIONARY → should MOVE
- After switch: penalty for MOVING → should STOP

The champion (all Stop) scores 9929 because it avoids ALL pre-switch penalty... wait, no. Before switch, score increases for STATIONARY robots. So Stop is BAD before switch!

Let me re-read: "Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary." So MOVING is better before switch. "From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving." So STOPPING is better after switch.

The champion all-Stop gets penalized before the switch (20 robots × ~500 steps = ~10000) but not after. That matches score ~9929!

The ideal: MOVE before switch, STOP after switch. Detect green → stop.

So the 2-state machines (Stop → Explore on green) are BACKWARDS! They start stopped (penalized before switch), then move when they see green (penalized after switch). That explains why they score worse.

The correct design: **Start MOVING, switch to STOP when green is detected**.

FSM: --nstates 1 --s0 1 --cle0 0

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Excellent! Now we have a clear picture:

**Champion**: GoToColor(green) → Stop on seeing green. Score 7521.83.
- State 0: B=8 (GoToColor green, clr=2) - moves toward green wall
- State 1: B=1 (Stop) - when green is seen, stop
- Transition: from state 0, when green perceived (C=7, l=2, p=0.9) → stop

This is brilliant: robots move toward the green wall (scoring 0 before switch, since they're moving), then when they see the green wall appear, they stop. But wait - the green wall only appears AFTER the switch. So before the switch, robots are moving (no penalty). After the switch, the green wall appears and robots detect it and stop (no penalty). 

The key insight: **GoToColor(green, clr=2) moves robots before the switch (good), and when green appears at the switch, C=7 triggers the stop (good)**. This is nearly optimal!

The score 7521 vs theoretical minimum: if all robots are moving pre-switch and all stop post-switch perfectly, score = 0. But detection has p=0.9 and there's reaction time, so some steps after switch robots are still moving.

**Exploitation strategy**:
- Try p=0.95, p=1.0 for the green detection (faster stopping)
- Try very low random-stop probability in state 1 (keep stopped)
- Try different movement behaviors in state 0 (Exploration, Repulsion, Attraction)
- Keep the stop probability in state 1 very low (0.001 is good)

**Key parameters to tune**:
1. Detection probability p0x0: higher = faster stop after switch. But could cause false positives? No - green only appears at switch, so higher p is better.
2. The `--p1x0 0.001` in stop state: very low probability of leaving stop (essentially never). Keep low.
3. Try clr=2 (green) confirmed as the right color.

Let me design 14 machines focusing on exploitation of the champion topology:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion is now clear: **GoToColor(green) with two transitions (p=1.0 on green detection AND tiny random transition) → Stop**. Score 6417.46.

Key findings:
1. **p=1.0 for green detection is better than p=0.9 or p=0.95** - the second transition with p=0.001 (tiny random stop) adds value
2. The champion has TWO transitions from state 0: green detection (p=1.0) AND random (p=0.001)
3. The random p=0.001 transition prevents any robot stuck without seeing green from staying in move state forever
4. GoToColor(green, clr=2) is the best movement behavior - it actively seeks the signal

**What to exploit:**
- The champion topology: `GoToColor(green) [p=1.0 green detect + p=0.001 random] → Stop [p=0.001 random → back]`
- Vary the random transition probability in state 0 (currently 0.001): try 0.0005, 0.002, 0.003
- Vary the stop-state exit probability (currently 0.001): try 0.0005, 0.0
- Try adding a second transition from Stop: if green disappears, go back to move? No - once they stop seeing green (if they've moved away), they should stay stopped. Keep stop exit very low.
- Try 3-state variants: GoToColor → Stop → (fallback)
- The `--n1x1 0 --c1x1 7 --p1x1 0.05 --l1x1 2` in rank 2 (stop→GoToColor when seeing green) seems to add value - if they see green and are stopped, stay stopped. But that transition goes back to GoToColor which is bad...

Actually rank 2 scored 6544 with the "if stopped and sees green, go to GoToColor(green)" which is slightly worse. So the simplest 2-state is best.

**Exploration ideas:**
- What if the random transition in state 0 is eliminated entirely (only green detection)? Try p=1.0 alone
- What if stop state has 0 transitions? Can't do that (--n must be ≥ 1? Actually no - let me check... the format requires --n<I> T with T in [1..4]. So minimum 1 transition)
- Try very tiny stop-exit: 0.0001
- Try adding neighbor-count condition in state 0 to detect clustering (robots cluster around green wall?)

Let me focus: the winner has `--n0 2` with green-detect (p=1.0) and random (p=0.001). The single-transition variants score ~8000. So the two-transition version is significantly better.

**Key variations to try:**
- Random prob in state 0: 0.0005, 0.001, 0.002, 0.003, 0.005
- Stop exit prob: 0.0005, 0.001, 0.002
- Add 3rd transition from state 0 (neighbor count?)
- Try with clr not 2 to confirm green is right (clr=2 seems right from B=8 GoToColor)

Wait - I should double-check: in the color system, clr=2 for GoToColor means the robot moves toward objects with color 2. The green wall uses color 2 (green). C=7 with l=2 also detects color 2. So both use the same color code. This is consistent.

## Reasoning

The champion is now: GoToColor(green) with two transitions (p=1.0 green detect, p=0.001 random) → Stop (p=0.0005 random exit). Score 5878.

Key pattern confirmed:
- State 0: B=8 GoToColor(clr=2), transitions: C=7 l=2 p=1.0 AND C=5 p=0.001
- State 1: B=1 Stop, transition: C=5 p=0.0005

The champion differs from rank 2 (6173) only in stop-exit probability: 0.0005 vs 0.001. Lower exit prob from stop is better.

**What to exploit:**
- Stop exit probability: try 0.0001, 0.0002, 0.0003, 0.0005 (champion), 0.001
- Random transition from state 0: try 0.0005, 0.001, 0.002 (champion has 0.001)
- The combination: p0x1=0.001 + p1x0=0.0005 is best so far

**What NOT to do:**
- Don't add neighbor-count conditions (rank 12-13 shows they hurt)
- Don't use Exploration instead of GoToColor (worse)
- Don't make stop exit too high

**Fine-tuning strategy:**
- Try p0x1 (random from GoToColor): 0.0005, 0.001, 0.0015, 0.002
- Try p1x0 (random from Stop): 0.0001, 0.0002, 0.0003, 0.0005
- Cross-combinations of these

The theoretical minimum: if all robots perfectly move before switch and stop after, score ≈ 0. The current score of ~5878 suggests some imperfection in detection timing and the random transitions causing occasional wrong-phase behavior.

The random p0x1=0.001 in GoToColor state means ~1.2 expected random transitions per robot over 600 pre-switch steps. This is causing some robots to stop early before the switch. Making it smaller (0.0005 or 0.0001) might help.

But wait - rank 11 has p0x1=0.0001 and scored 7129, worse than p0x1=0.001. So there's an optimal somewhere. The p0x1 random transition serves as a "safety" mechanism for robots that never see green.

Let me think: if a robot never detects green (bad viewing angle), it stays in GoToColor forever, accumulating score after the switch (moving when it shouldn't). The random transition at p=0.001 gives each robot ~0.001×700=0.7 expected stops per post-switch period - not very reliable. But the green detection at p=1.0 should catch most cases.

With p=0.0001, robots almost never randomly stop, so those that miss the green signal keep moving. With p=0.001, they have a chance. But p=0.001 also stops some robots before the switch (p=0.001 × 500 = 0.5 expected early stops per robot).

The sweet spot seems to be p0x1=0.001. Let me try very small values around there.

For stop exit (p1x0): lower is better - once stopped, stay stopped. 0.0005 > 0.001 in score, so try 0.0001, 0.0002.

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --cle0 0 --clr0 2 --n0 2 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 1 --l0x0 2 --n0x1 0 --c0x1 5 --p0x1 0.001 --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 5e-04

## Reasoning

The champion: p0x1=0.001 (random from GoToColor), p1x0=0.0001 (random from Stop). Score 5498.

Clear trend: **lower p1x0 (stop exit) is better**. The ranking shows:
- p1x0=0.0001 → 5498 (best)
- p1x0=0.0002 → 5576
- p1x0=0.0003 → 5544
- p1x0=0.0005 → 5710

For p0x1 (random from GoToColor state), p=0.001 is consistently best. Values of 0.0005 are worse (ranks 9-11).

**Key insight**: Lower p1x0 is better because robots that stop correctly (via green detection) should stay stopped. The limit is p1x0→0, but we must keep it >0 (minimum representable positive). Let's try p1x0=0.00005, 0.00001, 0.0001.

For p0x1, the sweet spot seems to be 0.001. Let me try values around it: 0.0008, 0.001, 0.0012, 0.0015.

Also notable: p0x1=0.002 + p1x0=0.0003 scored 5628, which is decent. The interaction matters.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion now has p1x0=0 (exactly zero exit probability from Stop state). Score 5170.93.

This confirms: **once stopped, never leave**. The stop state is a perfect absorbing state.

Now the only free parameter is p0x1 (random transition from GoToColor to Stop). The results show:
- p0x1=0.001 + p1x0=0 → 5170 (champion)
- p0x1=0.0011 + p1x0=0.0001 → 5308
- p0x1=0.0015 + p1x0=0.0001 → 5350
- p0x1=0.001 + p1x0=0.0001 → 5359

So p1x0=0 is confirmed best. Now I need to optimize p0x1.

The random transition p0x1 serves two purposes:
1. **Bad**: causes premature stops before the switch (costs score before switch)
2. **Good**: safety net for robots that never see green after the switch

With p0x1=0.001 over ~500 pre-switch steps: expected premature stops = 0.001 × 500 = 0.5 per robot, so ~10 robots stop early on average. That's costly.

But with p0x1=0, robots that can't see the green wall would stay in GoToColor forever after the switch. The green detection at p=1.0 should catch most robots that can see the wall.

Question: can I try p0x1=0? With 0 random exits and only green detection, robots that never see green keep moving forever. That could be worse if many robots miss the signal.

Let me try a range around 0.001: 0.0005, 0.0007, 0.0009, 0.001, 0.0011, 0.0013, 0.0015, 0.002.

Also try p0x1=0 (rely entirely on green detection).

The scores suggest the optimal p0x1 is around 0.001-0.0015. Let me densely sample this region with p1x0=0.

**All machines will use**: B=8 GoToColor(clr=2), p0x0=1.0 green detect, p1x0=0, varying p0x1.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The scores are now very close to each other - we're in a flat optimum region. The champion (5170.93) with p0x1=0.001 is essentially tied with p0x1=0.002 (5173.77) and p0x1=0.0015 (5178.26).

Key observations from round 8:
- p1x0=0 is confirmed best (absorbing stop state)
- p0x1 optimal range: 0.001-0.002, with a broad flat region
- The single-transition version (no random, only green detection) scores 7632 - much worse! This confirms the random transition is essential as a safety net
- 3-state machines perform worse (7706)

The trend shows: higher p0x1 (up to ~0.002) is slightly better, then degrades at 0.003. This is surprising - higher random transition from GoToColor means more premature stops before switch, but apparently the benefit of catching robots that miss green outweighs the cost.

Wait - let me reconsider. With p0x1=0.002 over 500 pre-switch steps: expected early stops = 0.002×500=1 per robot. That's 20 robot-steps penalty per robot on average from premature stops. But with p0x1=0.001: 0.5 per robot. Yet p0x1=0.002 is slightly better (5173 vs 5170 for champion at 0.001 - essentially same due to stochasticity).

The scores are so close (within noise) that we're at the optimum. Let me sample more densely around 0.001-0.003 with p1x0=0.

Also worth trying: p0x1=0.0025, 0.003 (already 5589 - worse), 0.0018, 0.0022.

The noise seems to dominate at this scale. Let me run multiple copies of the best configurations to get better statistics, and also try some wider exploration.

**Strategy for round 9:**
- Run champion verbatim (required)
- Run several near-champion variants with p0x1 in [0.001, 0.002] range
- Try p0x1=0.0017, 0.0018, 0.0019, 0.002, 0.0022, 0.0025
- One exploration: try adding a neighbor-count condition as additional safety
- Try slightly higher p0x1 values: 0.0025, 0.003

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
